\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 62.99 14.17 L 71.65 22.83}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 63.19 23.62 L 71.85 14.96}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 67.52 -18.7 M 73.42 -18.7 C 73.42 -15.44 70.78 -12.8 67.52 -12.8 C 64.26 -12.8 61.61 -15.44 61.61 -18.7 C 61.61 -21.96 64.26 -24.61 67.52 -24.61 C 70.78 -24.61 73.42 -21.96 73.42 -18.7 Z M 67.52 -18.7}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 62.99 -14.17 L 71.65 -22.83}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 63.19 -23.62 L 71.85 -14.96}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,+\mathcal{O}(\varphi^{4})\,,

(64)

where a single dashed line denotes the leading-order result,
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\chi}
. The second term on the right-hand side describes the dissipative process
\varphi\varphi\leftrightarrow\chi\chi
(when this process is kinematically allowed). The condensate EoM can then be written as

\displaystyle\left(\Box+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}\right)\varphi(x)+\frac{\lambda_{\phi}}{6}\varphi^{3}(x)+\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\Pi_{\varphi}^{r}(x,x^{\prime})\varphi(x^{\prime})+\frac{\varphi(x)}{6}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,V_{\varphi}^{r}(x-x^{\prime})\varphi^{2}(x^{\prime})=0\,,

(65)

where

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}(x)

\displaystyle=m_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{g}{2}\mathbf{\Delta}_{\chi}^{++}(0)+\frac{\lambda_{\phi}}{2}\Delta_{\phi}^{++}(x,x)\,,

(66)

\displaystyle V_{\varphi}^{r}(x-x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\mathrm{i}g^{2}}{2}\left[\left(\mathbf{\Delta}_{\chi}^{++}(x-x^{\prime})\right)^{2}-\left(\mathbf{\Delta}_{\chi}^{+-}(x-x^{\prime})\right)\right]\,.

(67)

Here
V_{\varphi}^{r}
is called the retarded proper four-vertex function, and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
is the leading-order frequency of the oscillation in multiple-scale analysis. Above, we have used the space-time-translation invariance for the thermal propagator,
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\chi}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathbf{\Delta}_{\chi}(x-x^{\prime})
. For
\Delta_{\phi}
, such property is absent because of the explicit
\varphi
-dependent mass for
\phi
particles and because they are not assumed to be in thermal equilibrium (which is the case of interest during freeze-in DM production).

In this work, for simplicity, we are interested in homogeneous scalar backgrounds, such as one obtained for the axion when spontaneous symmetry breaking occurs during inflation. Taking into account also the term
3H\dot{\varphi}
due to the expansion of the Universe, the EoM is:

\displaystyle\ddot{\varphi}(t)+3H\dot{\varphi}(t)+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}\varphi(t)+\frac{\lambda_{\phi}}{6}\varphi^{3}(t)+\int\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\pi_{\varphi}^{r}(t,t^{\prime})\varphi(t^{\prime})+\frac{\varphi(t)}{6}\int\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,v_{\varphi}^{r}(t-t^{\prime})\varphi^{2}(t^{\prime})=0\,,

(68)

where

\displaystyle\pi_{\varphi}^{r}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\,\Pi_{\varphi}^{r}(t,t^{\prime};\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\,,

(69)

\displaystyle v_{\varphi}^{r}(t-t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\,V_{\varphi}^{r}(t-t^{\prime};\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\,.

(70)

These terms introduce a non-locality in time. The term
\pi_{\varphi}^{r}(t,t^{\prime})
arises due to the condensate self-energy and interactions with
\phi
and
\chi
particles, whereas the term
v_{\varphi}^{r}(t-t^{\prime})
arises from interactions with only
\chi
particles. In addition, the effective mass is modified.

Multiple-scale analysis for the condensate EoM

Ref.
[
29
]
has analyzed the EoM in Eq. (
68
) using so-called multiple-scale analysis
[
43
,
44
]
in the case
\pi^{r}_{\varphi}(t,t^{\prime})=\pi^{r}_{\varphi}(t-t^{\prime})
, which applies when
\phi
-particles are in thermal equilibrium and
\Delta_{\phi}(x,x^{\prime})=\Delta_{\phi}(x-x^{\prime})
. Assuming that the two terms
\ddot{\varphi}
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}\varphi
are dominant in the EoM, this implies that

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}\gg\lambda_{\phi}\varphi^{2}\,,

(71)

which leads to a quasi-harmonic oscillation regime. As
M^{2}_{\phi}\;\raisebox{-3.00003pt}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\displaystyle>}}{{\sim}}$}\;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}
(because the last two terms in Eq. (
66
) correspond to only the first two diagrams in Eq. (
34
)), in this regime one also has

\displaystyle M^{2}_{\phi}\approx m_{\phi}^{2}+\Pi_{\phi}^{\mathcal{H}}\,.

(72)

That is, the
\varphi
-dependent mass can be neglected. For practical purposes, one can take the following approximation

\displaystyle M_{\phi}^{2}\approx\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}\approx m_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{g}{2}\mathbf{\Delta}_{\chi}^{++}(0)\overset{{\rm high}\,T}{=\joinrel=}m_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{g}{24}T^{2}\,,

(73)

where the term with
\Delta_{\phi}^{++}
has been neglected as
\phi
particles are far from equilibrium in the freeze-in scenario. Below, we neglect the minor difference between
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and
M_{\phi}
, and therefore do not distinguish
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
from
M_{\phi}
anymore. Now, we briefly introduce multiple-scale analysis. For more details, the reader is referred to Refs.
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
.

The idea of multiple-scale analysis is to realize that there is a hierarchy in the time scales in the evolution of the condensate. The shorter time scale corresponds to the oscillation frequency, which can also be understood as the microscopic time scale, and the longer time scale corresponds to the damping rate, which can be understood as the macroscopic time scale. The condensate evolution has the form
\varphi(t)=A(t)B(t)
where
B(t)
is an oscillating function and
A(t)
is the envelope function. It is expected that

\displaystyle M_{\phi}\sim\frac{1}{B(t)}\frac{\mathrm{d}B(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\gg\frac{1}{A(t)}\frac{\mathrm{d}A(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\,.

(74)

When taking time derivatives of
\varphi
, one can obtain quantities at different orders in magnitude. In order to trace these quantities, one can formally introduce two different time variables,
\varphi(t,\tau)=A(\tau)B(t)
with
\tau=\varepsilon t
where
\varepsilon
is only a bookkeeping parameter. Now we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial\varphi(t,\tau)}{\partial\tau}+\frac{\partial\varphi(t,\tau)}{\partial t}=\varepsilon\frac{\mathrm{d}A(\tau)}{\mathrm{d}\tau}B(t)+A(\tau)\frac{\mathrm{d}B(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\,.

(75)

This way, the smaller term is associated with the bookkeeping factor
\varepsilon
. Meanwhile, one shall assign an
\varepsilon
for each perturbation term in Eq. (
68
). Once the two-time scales have been introduced, we need to replace
\varphi(t)
in Eq. (
68
) with
\varphi(t,\tau)
, and
\varphi(t^{\prime})
with
\varphi(t^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
.

To localize the non-local terms in Eq. (
68
), one performs a Taylor expansion in the variable
\tau^{\prime}
at
\tau^{\prime}=\tau
. Now, the EoM in Eq. (
68
) can be solved perturbatively to the leading order in
\varepsilon
. The solution takes the following form:
10
10

10

If we distinguish
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
from
M_{\phi}
, then in Eq. (
76
)
M_{\phi}
would be replaced by
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu+f(t)
where
f(t)
also obeys an equation of motion
[
29
]
. The solution for
f(t)
should be close to
M_{\phi}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
in the limit of
\lambda_{\phi}=0
. For
\lambda_{\phi}\neq 0
,
f(t)
contains both the additional self-energy corrections and the correction from the term
\lambda_{\phi}\varphi^{3}/6
to the leading-order frequency in multiple-scale analysis, while
M_{\phi}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM_{\phi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
contains only the additional self-energy corrections.

\displaystyle\varphi(t)\approx A(t)\cos\left[\int^{t}M_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\right]\approx A(t)\cos\left(M_{\phi}(t)t\right)\,,

(76)

where an adiabatic change in
M_{\phi}(t)
is assumed. The EoM for
A(t)
reads
[
29
]

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}A(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}+\left(\gamma+\frac{3}{2}H(t)+\frac{1}{2M_{\phi}(t)}\frac{\mathrm{d}M_{\phi}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\right)A(t)+\frac{\sigma}{2}[A(t)]^{3}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(77)

where

\displaystyle\gamma\equiv-\frac{\text{Im}[\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{r}(M_{\phi})]}{2M_{\phi}}\,,\qquad\sigma\equiv-\frac{\text{Im}[\widetilde{v}_{\varphi}^{r}(2M_{\phi})]}{24M_{\phi}}\,,

(78)

with a tilde indicating the Fourier transform:

\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{r}(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega(t-t^{\prime})}\pi_{\varphi}^{r}(t-t^{\prime})\,,\qquad\widetilde{v}_{\varphi}^{r}(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega(t-t^{\prime})}v_{\varphi}^{r}(t-t^{\prime})\,.

(79)

Note that
\widetilde{\pi}^{r}_{\varphi}(\omega)=\widetilde{\Pi}^{r}_{\varphi}(\omega,\mathbf{k}=0)
and
\widetilde{v}^{r}_{\varphi}(\omega)=\widetilde{V}^{r}_{\varphi}(\omega,\mathbf{k}=0)
.

In our case,
\pi_{\varphi}^{r}(t,t^{\prime})\neq\pi^{r}_{\varphi}(t-t^{\prime})
, but one can still apply the above multiple-scale analysis. The only difference is that now we have the following replacement

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{r}(\omega)\rightarrow\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{r}(\omega,t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega(t-t^{\prime})}\pi_{\varphi}^{r}(t,t^{\prime})\,.

(80)

and thus
\tilde{\pi}^{r}_{\varphi}(M_{\phi})\rightarrow\tilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{r}(M_{\phi},t)
in the definition of
\gamma
in Eq. (
79
). Taking the inverse Wigner transform of
\pi_{\varphi}^{r}(t,t^{\prime})
inside the integral, one obtains

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{r}(t,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega(t-t^{\prime})}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega^{\prime}(t-t^{\prime})}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\left(\omega^{\prime},\frac{t+t^{\prime}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\left(\omega,\frac{t+t^{\prime}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\varphi}^{r}(\omega,t)-\frac{(t-t^{\prime})}{2}\partial_{t}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\omega,t)+\cdots\,.

(81)

Thus, at the leading order of the gradient expansion,
\widetilde{\pi}^{r}(t,\omega)
can be identified as the condensate self-energy in Winger space. While for
\tilde{v}^{r}_{\varphi}
we simply have
\widetilde{v}_{\varphi}^{r}(\omega)=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\varphi}^{r}(\omega)
due to
v^{r}_{\varphi}(t,t^{\prime})=v^{r}_{\varphi}(t-t^{\prime})
.

The imaginary parts of the retarded self-energy and proper four-vertex function of the condensate,
\gamma
and
\sigma
in Eqs. (
77
) and (
78
), have an interpretation in terms of microscopic processes. To see this, we note that (see e.g., Ref.
[
91
]
)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\varphi}^{r}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\varphi}^{>}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\varphi}^{<}\right)\,,

(82)

and similarly for the condensate proper four-vertex function,

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{r}_{\varphi}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{>}_{\varphi}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\varphi}^{<}\right)\,.

(83)

This has the same structure as the collision term in Eq. (
52
) except that there are no
\Delta^{<,>}_{\phi}
. Using the on-shell limit in Eq. (
57
) for
\Delta^{<,>}_{\phi}
(with
M_{\phi}
given by Eq. (
73
)) and Eq. (
63
) for
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\chi}^{<,>}
, one can see that
{\rm Im}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(t,M_{\phi})
describes all possible process with
\{\varphi\phi\phi\phi\}
or
\{\varphi\phi\chi\chi\}
where the condensate quantum has fixed four-momentum
\bar{k}\equiv(M_{\phi},\mathbf{0})
, provided the on-shell conditions can be satisfied (see Appendix
B
). For
{\rm Im}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\varphi}^{\rm R}(2M_{\phi})
, we have the process
\varphi\varphi\leftrightarrow\chi\chi

[
28
]
.

6
Boltzmann equations for particles and condensates

6.1
Recap for the simplified model

To put the condensate EoM (Eq. (
77
)) in the form of a Boltzmann equation, note that for the motion (
76
) if

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{A}}{A}\ll M_{\phi}\,,\quad\frac{\dot{M_{\phi}}}{M_{\phi}}\ll M_{\phi}\,,

(84)

which should be valid for most cases of interest,
the energy of the condensate can be approximated as

\displaystyle\rho_{\varphi}=\frac{1}{2}\dot{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}M_{\phi}^{2}\varphi^{2}\approx\frac{1}{2}M_{\phi}^{2}A(t)^{2}\,.

(85)

Defining
n_{\varphi}=\rho_{\varphi}/M_{\phi}
, we can rewrite Eq. (
77
) as

\displaystyle\dot{n}_{\varphi}+3Hn_{\varphi}=-2\gamma n_{\varphi}-\frac{2\sigma}{M_{\phi}}n_{\varphi}^{2}\,.

(86)

Note that the term with
\dot{M_{\phi}}/M_{\phi}
from Eq. (
77
) is cancelled by the same term from the derivative of
n_{\varphi}
. Thus, we recover the well-known result for the QCD axion that the number density (not the energy density) is covariantly conserved in the presence of a slowly varying particle mass, once condensate oscillations begin in the regime
M_{\phi}\gg H
. We further define the following collision operators

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\pi}=-2\gamma=\frac{{\rm Im}\,\bar{\pi}^{r}_{\varphi}[M_{\phi}]}{M_{\phi}}\,,\qquad\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{v}=-\frac{\sigma}{M_{\phi}}=\frac{{\rm Im}\,\bar{v}^{r}_{\varphi}[2M_{\phi}]}{24M^{2}_{\phi}}\,.

(87)

Then Eq. (
77
) finally becomes Eq. (
3a
). Explicitly,
\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\pi/v}
take the form

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\pi/v}[f_{i}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\rm all\ possible\ processes}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2M_{\phi}\times(2M_{\phi})^{n-1}}\int\prod_{i}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2k_{i}^{0}}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(n\bar{k}+k_{A1}+\cdots-k_{B1}-\cdots)

\displaystyle\times\{(1\pm f_{A1})\cdots f_{B1}\cdots-f_{A1}\cdots(1\pm f_{B1})\cdots\}\,|\mathcal{M}_{(n\varphi)A_{1}\cdots\rightarrow B_{1}\cdots}|^{2}\Bigg]\,,

(88)

where
\bar{k}=(M_{\phi},\mathbf{0})
is the four-momentum of condensate quanta, and
n
counts how many condensate quanta are involved in the interaction. For instance,
n=1
for
\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\pi}
,
n=2
for
\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{v}
. At tree-level,
\mathcal{M}_{(n\varphi)A_{1}\cdots\rightarrow B_{1}\cdots}
is read from the Lagrangian by replacing
\varphi
with
1
. For example, for the term
g\varphi^{2}\chi^{2}/4
, we have
|\mathcal{M}_{(2\varphi)\rightarrow\chi\chi}|^{2}=1/2\times(g/2)^{2}
where the particular factor of
1/2
is due to the exchange symmetry of the produced two
\chi
-particles.

Now let us also formulate the particle Boltzmann equation in Eq. (
61
) in terms of the particle density. We initiate our analysis with the first term on the left-hand side,
k_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}_{(x)}f_{\phi}(k,t)/k^{0}
.
Since the condensate background is homogeneous, we expect that
f_{\phi}(k,x)=f_{\phi}(k^{0},t)
. After imposing the on-shell condition, we simply have

\frac{1}{k^{0}}k_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}_{(x)}f_{\phi}(k,t)\rightarrow\frac{\partial f_{\phi}(E,t)}{\partial t}\,,

(89)

where
E=\sqrt{M_{\phi}^{2}+\mathbf{k}^{2}}
. One can also view
f_{\phi}
as a function of
|\mathbf{k}|
,
f_{\phi}(|\mathbf{k}|,t)
.
So far, we have not taken into account the expansion of the Universe in this equation. As commonly done in the literature, we only take into account the effect of the Universe expansion in the kinetic term (the first term) of Eq. (
61
) while the collision terms are computed in flat spacetime. This artificial combination is partly due to difficulties in deriving the dynamics in a curved spacetime background from the beginning but also is arguably valid since the scale of microscopic processes is much smaller than the Hubble scale and the impact of the expansion of the Universe can be neglected. To do that, we do the following replacement
[
92
]

\frac{\partial f_{\phi}(|\mathbf{k}|,t)}{\partial t}\rightarrow\frac{\partial f_{\phi}(|\mathbf{k}|,t)}{\partial t}-H|\mathbf{k}|\frac{\partial f_{\phi}(|\mathbf{k}|,t)}{\partial|\mathbf{k}|}\,.

(90)

After invoking the definition

\displaystyle n_{\phi}(t)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,f_{\phi}(|\mathbf{k}|,t)\,,

(91)

and integrating Eq. (
90
) yields

\dot{n}_{\phi}(t)+3Hn_{\phi}-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{E}\frac{\mathrm{d}M_{\phi}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial f_{\phi}(E,t)}{\partial E}\right)\,,

(92)

where the last term is due to
M_{\phi}
being time dependent. This term will be cancelled by the second term in Eq. (
61
) that we discuss below.
We assume that the expansion of the Universe does not significantly modify the second and the collision term in Eq. (
61
).

The second term on the left-hand side of Eq. (
61
) is usually identified as a force term by writing

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial^{(x)}_{\mu}M_{\phi}^{2})=M_{\phi}F_{\mu}\,,\quad F_{\mu}\equiv\frac{\partial M_{\phi}}{\partial x^{\mu}}\,.

(93)

This force is due to the spacetime dependence of the particle mass under study. In our case, due to the assumption of a homogeneous background, we simply have

\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{E}\frac{\mathrm{d}M_{\phi}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial f_{\phi}(E,t)}{\partial E}\,.

(94)

Doing the integral over
\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}
, we get a term that is exactly cancelled by the last term in Eq. (
92
). One then finally get Eq. (
3b
). Generally, the right-hand side can only be discussed on a case-by-case basis. Comparing this equation with Eq. (
3a
), we see that in deriving the latter, there is no need to do an integral over the momentum space. This is reasonable as the condensate is cold and all condensate quanta have a fixed, unique four-momentum
\bar{k}=(M_{\phi},\mathbf{0})
. However, if one wishes, he/she may note that condensate quanta have a singular distribution function given by

\displaystyle f_{\varphi}(\mathbf{k},t)=n_{\varphi}(t)(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{k})\,,

(95)

which cannot be obtained from the Bose-Einstein distribution in the zero-temperature limit.

Until now, we have not been concerned with the collision terms that contain explicit factors of
\varphi
in the particle Boltzmann equation. We analyze this in the next subsection.

6.2
Collision terms in the presence of condensates

Now we consider the
\phi
particle self-energies involving the condensate, i.e., the third and fourth diagrams in Eq. (
34
), or equivalently, the third and fourth terms in Eq. (
4
). We consider the quasi-harmonic oscillation regime in which the background field can be written as (c.f. Eq. (
76
))

\displaystyle\varphi(t)=\frac{A(t)}{2}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bar{k}x}+\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bar{k}x}\right)\,.

(96)

Recall that
\bar{k}=(M_{\phi},\mathbf{0})
.
To be specific, let us consider the fourth term in Eq. (
4
), namely

\displaystyle\Pi_{\phi,(4)}^{ab}(x_{1},x_{2})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}g^{2}}{2}\varphi(x_{1})[\Delta_{\chi}^{ab}(x_{1},x_{2})]^{2}\varphi(x_{2})\equiv\varphi(x_{1})\mathbf{\Pi}^{ab}_{\phi,(4)}(x_{1},x_{2})\varphi(x_{2})\,,

(97)

where we have defined
\mathbf{\Pi}^{ab}_{\phi,(4)}
; we refer to it as the
reduced self-energy
. We have suppressed the Keldysh index on
\varphi
as in the EoMs, one would take
\varphi^{+}=\varphi^{-}=\varphi
in the end. One can also define the reduced self-energy for the third term in Eq. (
4
), i.e.,
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\phi,(3)}^{ab}
.

When performing the Wigner transform for
\Pi^{<,>}_{\phi,(4)}(x_{1},x_{2})
, we have a factor in the integral that looks like

\displaystyle\varphi\left(x+\frac{r}{2}\right)\varphi\left(x-\frac{r}{2}\right)=\frac{A^{2}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\partial^{2})}{4}\left[\cos(2M_\phi t)+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bar{k}r}+\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bar{k}r}\right]\,.

(98)

The self-energy in Wigner space reads

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\phi,(4)}^{<,>}(k,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{A^{2}(t)\cos(2M_\phi t)}{4}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbf{\Pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\phi,(4)}^{<,>}(k,x)+\frac{A^{2}(t)}{4}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbf{\Pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\phi,(4)}^{<,>}(k+\bar{k},x)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{A^{2}(t)}{4}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbf{\Pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\phi,(4)}^{<,>}(k-\bar{k},x)\,.

(99)

Substituting the above into the right-hand side of Eq. (
52
) in the on-shell limit, one then obtains three different types of collision terms:

(a)

Condensate modified vertex
: The first term in Eq. (
6.2
) gives collision terms with two
\chi
particles and one
\phi
particle. The squared transition amplitude is multiplied by
A^{2}(t)\cos(2M_\phi t)/4
compared with that when
\varphi(t)=1
. If one takes the cycle average, this collision term vanishes.

(b)

Absorbing one condensate quantum
: The second term describes a condensate quantum absorbed by particles. The squared transition amplitude is enhanced by a factor of
A^{2}(t)/4
compared to that when
\varphi(t)=1
. We denote this type of collision term as
\mathcal{C}_{(+\varphi)}[f_{\phi},f_{i}]
. If we define

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\phi\varphi A_{1}\cdots\rightarrow B_{1}\cdots}[f_{\phi},f_{i}]=\sum\Bigg[\frac{1}{2k^{0}\times(2M_{\phi})}\int\prod_{i}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}k_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2k_{i}^{0}}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(k+\bar{k}+k_{A1}+\cdots-k_{B1}-\cdots)

\displaystyle\times\{(1\pm f_{\phi})(1\pm f_{A1})\cdots f_{B1}\cdots-f_{\phi}f_{A1}\cdots(1\pm f_{B1})\cdots\}\,|\mathcal{M}_{\phi\varphi A_{1}\cdots\rightarrow B_{1}\cdots}|^{2}\Bigg]\,,

(100)

where
|\mathcal{M}_{\phi\varphi...\rightarrow...}|
is defined as described below Eq. (
6.1
) such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{(+\varphi)}[f_{\phi},f_{i}]=n_{\varphi}\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\phi\varphi A_{1}\cdots\rightarrow B_{1}\cdots}\,.

(101)

(c)

Emitting one condensate quantum
: Similar to the last case, the third term in Eq. (
6.2
) describes a condensate quantum emitted by particles. We denote this type of collision term as
\mathcal{C}_{(-\varphi)}[f_{\phi},f_{i}]
. Similarly, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{(-\varphi)}[f_{\phi},f_{i}]=n_{\varphi}\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\phi A_{1}\cdots\rightarrow\varphi B_{1}\cdots}\,.

(102)

In all the above, we observe Bose enhancement of the collision term by the condensate number density,
n_{\varphi}
.

6.3
Generalisation: the Primakoff interaction

Although the preceding analysis is based on the simple model in Eq. (
4
), the results are general. Here, we apply our results to an axion-like model. Consider the Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\Phi\partial^{\mu}\Phi-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\Phi^{2}-\frac{1}{4!}\lambda\Phi^{4}-\frac{g}{4}\Phi F_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\,,

(103)

where
F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}
and
\widetilde{F}
is its Hodge dual.
We assume that the axion field
\Phi
forms a condensate,
\langle\Phi\rangle\equiv\varphi
, whose oscillation is quasi-harmonic. Substituting
\Phi=\varphi+\phi
into the Lagrangian, one obtains

\mathcal{L}=\bar{\mathcal{L}}[\varphi]+\hat{\mathcal{L}}[\phi;\varphi]
where

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}[\varphi]=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\mu}\varphi)\partial^{\mu}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\varphi^{2}-\frac{1}{4!}\lambda\varphi^{4}\,,

(104)

and

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{L}}[\phi;\varphi]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\mu}\phi)\partial^{\mu}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\left(m^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\varphi^{2}\right)\phi^{2}-\frac{\lambda}{3!}\varphi\phi^{3}-\frac{1}{4!}\lambda\phi^{4}-\frac{g}{4}\phi F_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}-\frac{g}{4}\varphi F_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+({\rm linear\ terms\ in\ fluctuations})\,.

(105)

We also need the interaction between photons and electrons

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm SM}\supset\bar{\psi}(\mathrm{i}\not{\partial}-m+e\not{A})\psi\,.

(106)

Properly truncating the effective action, we have (only diagrams with at least one condensate quantum or
\phi
propagator are shown)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}=-\mathrm{i}\left(\,\hbox to57.31pt{\vbox to28.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.65276pt\lower-14.42638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

) but without the

(109)

For the third and fourth diagrams in Eq. (
107
), we simply replaced the double snaked lines in Eq. (
107
) with single snaked lines after the small-field expansion.

Condensate Boltzmann equation

For the collision terms in the condensate Boltzmann equation in Eq. (
3a
), we simply cut the second diagram in Eq. (
107
) and the diagrams in Eqs. (
108
) and (
109
), see Appendix
B
. For the first two diagrams, the kinematically allowed processes are the pure decay
\varphi\rightarrow\gamma\gamma
and scattering process
\varphi\phi\rightarrow\phi\phi
, and their inverse processes. They belong to the
\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\pi}
type interactions since only one condensate quantum is involved. One can write down the collision terms following Eq. (
6.1
). The only difference one needs to take care of is the polarization of the photons and the spinor structure of electrons in writing down
\mathcal{M}
. This complication also appears for conventional Boltzmann equations in the absence of condensates and we do not discuss it further. After cutting the diagram in Eq. (
109
), we obtain the Primakoff process with the axion
particle
replaced by an axion condensate
quantum
,
\gamma e\rightarrow\varphi e
and its inverse process. Of course, one also has the scattering
\varphi\gamma\leftrightarrow\bar{e}e
and the decay process
\varphi\leftrightarrow\bar{e}e\gamma
(if kinematically allowed).

Through the above processes, condensate quanta can be created and annihilated. One may naively conclude that the Primakoff interaction, for example, could create an equilibrium number density of both particles
and
condensate. However, generically, it is expected that the total effect will lead to a decrease in the total condensate quantum number, leading to the dissipation of the condensate energy into the plasma. This expectation is based on the fact that the cold condensate has zero entropy and therefore, thermodynamically, dissipation and thermalization occur instead of the reverse. This is also explicitly confirmed in the studies of the evolution of oscillating condensates using the model in Eq. (
4
)
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
, as well as in studies on reheating.

Particle Boltzmann equation

For the collision terms in the particle Boltzmann equation in Eq. (
61
), we look at the last three diagrams in Eq. (
107
) which gives

where we have kept the external legs, which do not contribute to the explicit expression of the self-energy, for the reason of applying the cutting rules (see Appendix
B
). Cutting the last two diagrams gives the decay process
\phi\leftrightarrow\gamma\gamma
,
\phi\leftrightarrow\gamma\bar{e}e
(if kinematically allowed), and scattering
\phi\gamma\leftrightarrow\bar{e}e
,
\gamma e\leftrightarrow\phi e
(the Primarkoff process). The process corresponding to the first diagram has been discussed in the two-scalar model in Eq. (
4
).

7
Summary and Conclusions

In this paper, we have derived from first principles of non-equilibrium quantum field theory the coupled equations describing the interaction between particles and an oscillating homogeneous condensate for a scalar field
\Phi
in the plasma of the early Universe. This formalism can be used to describe the production of scalar DM, including axions, in the form of both particles, for which an irreducible population is produced by freeze-in through portal interactions with the Standard Model, and of a condensate, which arises due to spontaneous symmetry breaking and/or inflationary fluctuations. The condensate and particles are described by the one- and two-point functions, respectively.

The fundamental non-local EoMs, which in principle were known prior to our work, are, however, very difficult to apply in practice to address questions about the DM relic density. This is due to the equations being non-local (and thus insusceptible to standard numerical solution methods), and due to the technically difficult formalism, which may obscure the essential physics relevant for DM. The derivation of the Boltzmann equation in the absence of condensates is known in the literature
[
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
. However, including the condensate in this derivation is, to the best of our knowledge, lacking. We thus presented a novel simultaneous Markovianization of the quantum equations for both the one- and two-point functions. The gradient expansion is not sufficient to localize the EoM of the oscillating condensate and a key additional input is the use of multiple-scale analysis introduced in Refs.
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
to solve non-equilibrium QFT problems. This Markovianization process, surprisingly, results in coupled Boltzmann equations for
both
the condensate and particles with explicit local collision operators. Thus, they can be easily applied to studying the DM relic density.

Studies on the evolution of axion condensates using the CTP formalism have been carried out in Refs.
[
45
,
46
]
recently. Similarly to Refs.
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
, these works assume that the condensate self-energies
\Pi_{\varphi}
depend on two-point functions of thermal bath fields only, i.e., fields that are in thermal equilibrium. This way, the condensate EoM can be solved independently. Since
\Pi_{\varphi}
depends on
\Delta_{\phi}
also, in that computation one thus has to assume a thermal equilibrium form for
\Delta_{\phi}
. Equivalently, the (axion) DM
particles
are assumed to be in thermal equilibrium with the plasma. Therefore, those results cannot be applied to the freeze-in scenario of DM production. The latter is precisely the motivation of the present work. Hence, in the present work we have to solve the coupled EoMs for the one- and two-point functions simultaneously, i.e. making no assumption on the form of
\Delta_{\phi}
. We also note that recently the CTP formalism has been used to derive the transport equation of photons in Magnetised Plasmas with axion sources
[
93
]
.

The highly non-trivial non-locality of the quantum EoMs is expressed by the infinite number of derivatives needed to describe them in the gradient expansion. This can be contrasted to a more trivial form of non-locality in the classical equations for a self-gravitating condensate. The condensate “wavefunction”,
\psi(x,t)
, obeys a non-local (in space) Schrödinger equation, where the potential is given by the long-range Newtonian self-gravity of the condensate
[
94
]
. The non-locality can be trivially accounted for by introducing a local second-order auxiliary equation for the gravitational potential: Poisson’s equation. However, the Newtonian potential introduces no new dynamical degrees of freedom. The coupled equations are local and the system can then be solved by leap-frog spectral methods or finite differences at finite order in derivatives (e.g., Refs.
[
95
,
96
,
97
]
). In this treatment, the fluctuations upon the condensate have not been accounted for, which makes the condensate EoM local.

Such a simplification cannot be obtained for the full quantum non-locality in QFT. For example, a QFT system is in principle described by a
wavefunctional
instead of a wavefunction. Specifically, to fully capture all the information in a QFT, one in principle needs all
n
-point functions which are non-local for
n>1
, although, for most practical purposes, we only need to study the EoMs for the one- and two-point functions. Still, their EoMs involve non-local integrals involving two-point functions resulting from interactions among particles, or between particles and the condensate, that are characterized by various self-energies. As well as being non-local in time, the quantum equations with infinite derivatives also have a problem concerning the Cauchy data. The process of Markovianization we developed here allows these problems to be bypassed in a controlled manner.
11
11

11

We thank Dima Levkov for the discussion on these points.

Our results may find applications in DM production during perturbative reheating
[
98
,
99
,
100
,
101
,
102
]
, although particle production, in that case, may be dominated by the earlier nonperturbative preheating stage
[
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
,
107
,
108
,
109
,
110
]
. We emphasize that our derivation assumes that the condensate oscillates quasi-harmonically. For more general oscillations, a first-principles derivation of the dynamics would require further developments in multiple-scale analysis applied to solving the non-local condensate EoM. For DM production in situations other than reheating, however, quasi-harmonically condensates should be a generic enough assumption.

In this work, we have presented only the formalism to describe the interaction between particles and condensates during DM production. In a follow-up paper, we will numerically solve the coupled equations. The condensate can transfer energy to the particles, and condensate quanta are also created in particle processes. In applications with multiple-component DM, these effects could be significant, especially in systems involving resonances and level-crossing, such as those explored in relation to the condensate dynamics alone in Refs.
[
111
,
112
,
113
]
.

Above, the use of a double dashed line is to emphasize that the
\chi
propagator implicitly depends on
\varphi
due to the modified dispersion relation in Eq. (
29b
). In Sec.
5.2
, we discuss the expansion of
\Delta_{\chi}[\varphi]
in
\varphi
, the small-field expansion from Ref.
[
27
]
, and we use the single dashed line for
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\chi}\equiv\Delta_{\chi}[\varphi=0]
. For
\Delta_{\phi}
, we do not need to do such an expansion since we solve the EoM of
\Delta_{\phi}
anyway.

A diagram is called one-particle-reducible if it can be separated into two disconnected parts by cutting one propagator. Such diagrams are generated by interaction terms linear in the fluctuation fields. An example is shown in Eq. (
26
). A diagram is called two-particle-reducible if it can be separated into two disconnected parts by cutting two propagators. An example is the following,

It is separated into two disconnected parts if one cuts the two propagators of the middle loop. In 2PI effective actions, one only considers two-particle-
irreducible
diagrams in
\Gamma_{2}
which represents the contribution to the effective action
\Gamma_{\rm 2PI}
starting from two loops.

Appendix B
Cutting rules

In this appendix, we illustrate the cutting rules in more detail using some examples.

B.1
Particle kinetic equation

We first consider how to get the collision term in Eq. (
61
) from Eq. (
52
). Although this is a simple mathematical exercise by simply substituting the on-shell Wightman functions in Wigner space into Eq. (
52
). It is nonetheless helpful to write down the collision terms based on some rules that can be illustrated with Feynman diagrams.

Without condensate

First, we start with a diagram in
\Gamma_{2}
, for e.g., the sixth diagram in Eq. (
4
) (take the leading order after the small-field expansion in
\Delta_{\chi}
),

Note that in
\Pi_{\phi,(6)}
, there are three propagators (c.f. Eq. (
5.1
)). Thus, we see three particles corresponding to it. Now we see that the external legs that we kept in Eq. (
116
) correspond to, after cutting the diagram, the
\phi
particle from
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Delta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\phi}^{<,>}
in Eq. (
52
).

The collision term actually contains more processes that allow the particles from the cut loop to reverse their role from being an outgoing particle to being an incoming particle. This is essentially due to the relations in Eqs. (
59
) and (
60
). Physically, this is due to the fact that the plasma can provide incoming particles, which is different from the case at zero temperature. In conclusion, for
\Pi_{\phi,(6)}
, we have the cutting rules:

(119)

Again, we have kept the external legs in the diagram for the self-energy. One then cuts the loop in the self-energy diagram. Now, we also have two possibilities for the condensate quantum: incoming condensate quantum or outgoing condensate quantum, corresponding to the absorption and emission cases discussed in section
6.2
. Note the first type discussed there, where the condensate only modifies the vertex amplitude, does not contribute if one takes the cycle average, and therefore we do not consider it.

In conclusion, for the self-energy
\Pi_{\phi,(4)}
, we have the following cutting rules:

(120)

The first/last two diagrams represent the emission/absorption of a condensate quantum by the
\phi
particle. Note that not all the processes are possible; one has to make sure that they are kinematically allowed.

B.2
Condensate kinetic equation

We now consider the kinetic equation for the condensate, Eq. (
3a
). Again, we use the fourth diagram in Eq. (
4
) to illustrate the cutting rules. The condensate self-energy (
\Pi_{\varphi}
) corresponding to this diagram is obtained by cutting one condensate quantum. However, to illustrate the cutting rule for the condensate dissipation, we should still use the diagram in the effective action (this is similar to how we kept the external legs in drawing the diagrams for
\Pi_{\phi}
). Then the collision terms in Eq. (
3a
) (corresponding to
\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\pi}
) can be obtained from Eq. (
82
) through the following cutting rule:
